Completely back in it… no, I don’t think I’d say it’s the whole scene like that. Not like I lose track of where I am entirely or go through the whole thing from beginning to end.

It’s more… out of nowhere I’ll see his hand again, holding that heavy tool. Just that piece of it. It comes into my mind like a picture popping up when I didn’t ask for it. I might be shaving, just doing something ordinary, or working on a painting and trying to concentrate, and then there it is. His hand, the tool, that feeling of weight to it.

And even though I know I’m in my bathroom or sitting with my paints, my chest still tightens. So it isn’t the whole scene exactly, but that one image feels strong enough that it can knock me off balance for a bit.

What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Well… I guess the plain answer is I’m here because I’m tired of avoiding things. I keep finding ways not to go into my own workshop, which used to be my place, you know, where I’d spend hours. And I’ve been steering clear of family get-togethers too, if there’s any chance my brother-in-law might be there.\n\nIt’s gotten to where I’m planning around it all the time—who’s going to be where, whether I can leave early, whether I should just not go. I haven’t really noticed it happening that way.\n\nFor me it’s more that I see a situation coming and I start thinking around it ahead of time, if that makes sense. Not like out of nowhere I’m hit with terror. At least, not that I can clearly point to.\n\nDoctor: Is there something specific about your brother-in-law or your workshop that you're trying to avoid?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I suppose there is, though I don’t always like saying it that plainly. Those two things—my workshop and being around my brother-in-law at family gatherings—they remind me of things I’ve been trying not to sit with.\n\nSo what I end up doing is steering around them. If there’s a birthday or holiday or someone says, “Oh, he might stop by,” I start thinking about whether I can skip it, or show up late, or leave early, or ask who exactly is coming. And with the workshop, I’ll find some excuse not to go in there, even though it used to be where I felt comfortable. I’ll tell myself I’ll do it tomorrow, or I don’t need whatever tool is in there that badly.\n\nAfter all this time, it feels almost foolish to admit how much I plan my day around avoiding those reminders. But yes, that’s really what it is. I’m trying not to run into what they bring up in me.\n\nDoctor: What exactly does your brother-in-law or the workshop remind you of that you're trying to avoid?\n\nPatient: It reminds me of… his hand, mostly. That sounds strange, maybe, because you’d think I’d say the whole situation or the argument or the room or whatever, but what comes back is his hand holding that heavy tool.\n\nIt’s not like I sit down and decide to remember it. It’ll just pop into my head out of nowhere, like a picture getting shoved in front of me. I’ll be shaving, or I’ll be working on a painting, just doing some ordinary thing, and suddenly I can see it again—his hand wrapped around it. And then my chest tightens up before I’ve even had time to think much.\n\nSo when I know he might be at a gathering, or when I think about going into the workshop where tools are sitting around, I guess I’m trying to avoid having that picture come back. Because it still does. Even now.\n\nDoctor: Was there a specific traumatic event involving your brother-in-law and the heavy tool that you're remembering?\n\nPatient: I… I don’t know how much I can lay it all out cleanly. What I can say is that what keeps coming back is that image—his hand with that heavy tool in it. That’s the part my mind seems to grab onto.\n\nIt isn’t like I’m sitting there trying to remember some whole scene from start to finish. It’s more sudden than that. I’ll be doing something ordinary, like shaving in the morning, or working on one of my paintings, and out of nowhere I’ll see it again, just like a picture popping into my head. His hand around that tool. And the strange thing is, it can happen so fast that my body reacts before I’ve really thought anything through—my chest tightens up, and I feel like I’m right back with that image for a moment.\n\nSo yes, there was something about that tool and him that stuck with me, but the part I keep remembering most clearly is that hand holding it. That’s what I’ve been trying to stay away from, I guess.\n\nDoctor: Did something bad happen involving your brother-in-law and the heavy tool?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I can say it that directly, not because I’m trying to be difficult, but because when you put it that way—“something bad happened”—my mind sort of locks up. I know what I remember, and I know what I react to. I remember him with that tool in his hand, and I remember how awful it feels when that picture comes back.\n\nBut I don’t have a clean, neat answer beyond that right now. It’s like I can get close to it, circle around it, describe the edges of it, but the minute I try to say exactly what happened, everything gets jumbled and I want to back away from it. So… I’m not saying nothing happened. I’m saying I’m not sure I can give you the full thing in words right this second.\n\nWhat I do know is that seeing him, or even thinking about that workshop, brings me right back to that image. And that’s the part I’ve been trying so hard not to face.\n\nDoctor: Did your brother-in-law use the heavy tool in a way that hurt you?\n\nPatient: I… that’s where it gets difficult for me to answer straight. I remember the shouting. I remember him having the tool, and I remember it so clearly it’s almost too clear, like that part is burned in. I can see his hand around it, the way he was standing there, the feeling in the room.\n\nBut right around how it actually ended… there’s a blank. Not the whole thing, just a piece of it. Like somebody took a few minutes and cut them out of the story. I can get up to a certain point in my mind, and then the next thing I know I’m past it somehow, but I don’t have the middle.\n\nSo when you ask if he used it in a way that hurt me, I don’t want to say something I can’t fully hold onto. I know I was scared, and I know that tool is what my mind keeps dragging back up. But the exact part where it ended, or what happened in those few minutes, is just… not there clearly.\n\nDoctor: Did your brother-in-law physically hurt you with the heavy tool?\n\nPatient: I can’t honestly say it in that exact way, because that’s the part that goes missing on me. I know that probably sounds evasive, and I’m not trying to dodge you. I remember the shouting very clearly, and I remember the tool in his hand—too clearly, really. That part is sharp in my mind.\n\nBut right around how it actually ended, there’s just this blank place. It’s like I can follow the memory up to a certain point, and then a few minutes are just gone, like somebody cut them out of the story. So when you ask if he physically hurt me with it, I don’t have a clean yes or no that I can stand behind. I remember being there, I remember him with it, I remember the noise and the fear around it… but the exact ending, the part you’re asking about, isn’t there plain enough for me to say.\n\nDoctor: Do you recall experiencing any physical pain or injury during the incident with your brother-in-law and the heavy tool?\n\nPatient: I wish I could answer that more plainly. I know you’re asking if I remember pain, like a hit or an injury right then, but that’s exactly where my memory kind of drops out.\n\nI remember the shouting. I remember the tool in his hand clear as anything—too clear, really. I can still picture that part. But right around how it actually ended, there’s this blank spot, like a few minutes got cut out of the story. I can get up to that point, and then it’s just… gone, or covered over somehow.\n\nSo I don’t want to say yes, I remember pain, because I don’t have that part clear. But I also can’t say no like I’m certain nothing happened. The part where I’d know that is the part I can’t seem to reach.\n\nDoctor: Did something traumatic happen to you on the day you remember your brother-in-law with the heavy tool?\n\nPatient: I don’t know how to answer that cleanly. I mean… something happened that day that has stayed with me, that much I can say. I remember my brother-in-law there, and I remember the heavy tool, and I remember feeling like everything in the room had gotten dangerous. It wasn’t just an ordinary argument in my mind.\n\nBut if you’re asking me to say exactly what the traumatic part was, or whether I can lay it out from start to finish, I can’t. That’s where I get stuck. I have pieces of it, and some of those pieces are very sharp, but then there’s that missing stretch where I can’t pull it together.\n\nSo… yes, in the sense that the day felt traumatic to me and it still does when I think about it. But I’m not sure I can give you the full picture of what happened, not in a way that feels honest.\n\nDoctor: Did the incident with your brother-in-law and the heavy tool involve any physical contact with you?\n\nPatient: I can’t say for certain whether there was physical contact. That’s the part that bothers me, because I feel like I ought to be able to answer a simple question like that, but I can’t honestly fill in that piece.\n\nI remember the shouting, and I remember the heavy tool in his hand plain enough. Those parts are right there, almost too clear. But right around how it actually ended, it just goes blank on me. It’s like a few minutes got cut out of the story, and that missing part is exactly where I’d be able to tell you whether he touched me or hit me or anything like that.\n\nSo I don’t want to tell you yes if I’m not sure, and I don’t want to tell you no like I know nothing happened. I just… I don’t have that part.\n\nDoctor: Do you believe that what happened with your brother-in-law is the main reason you've been avoiding your workshop and family gatherings?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think so. I mean, I don’t like saying it that directly, because then it sounds like I’ve made some neat little connection and solved it, and it doesn’t feel neat to me. But yes, that’s a big part of why I came in.\n\nI keep finding reasons not to go into my own workshop, which is ridiculous in a way, because that used to be my space. I don’t have to explain it to anybody there, I don’t have to make conversation, I can just putter around and fix things. But now I’ll stand there thinking about going in, and then I’ll tell myself I’ll do it later, or the light isn’t good, or I don’t need that tool today, or whatever excuse comes to mind. And the family gatherings are the same kind of thing, maybe worse, because if there’s even a chance my brother-in-law might be there, I start planning around it before I even admit that’s what I’m doing.\n\nAfter all this time, I’m tired of arranging my life so I don’t run into anything that reminds me of that day. I’m tired of steering clear of places and people like it’s normal. So yes, I believe what happened with him is probably the main reason, or at least the reason that makes those places feel so hard to face now.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever find yourself reliving the moment when you saw your brother-in-law with the heavy tool, like it's happening all over again?\n\nPatient: Yes… not always like I’m completely back there, if that makes sense, but there are times it comes up so strong that it feels like my body is reacting before I’ve even had a chance to think.\n\nIt’ll happen out of nowhere. I can be shaving in the morning, just looking at my own face in the mirror, or I’ll be working on a painting and concentrating on a color or a line, and suddenly I’ll see his hand again with that heavy tool in it. Not the whole day, not a clear beginning-to-end thing, just that one picture. His hand, the weight of it, the sense of it being there. And it pops into my head like somebody slid a photograph in front of me.\n\nWhen that happens, my chest tightens up. I’ll stop what I’m doing for a second, sometimes longer, because it doesn’t feel like just a normal memory. It has that feeling of “there it is again,” like I didn’t choose to think about it and I can’t quite push it away fast enough. So… yes, I do relive that part, at least that image of it. Not neatly, not the whole thing, but enough that it still gets to me.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel like you're back in the moment when your brother-in-law had the heavy tool, reliving it as if it's happening again?\n\nPatient: Yes… I suppose that’s the closest way to put it, though it isn’t always like I’m fully standing there again from start to finish. It’s more that one part of it comes back at me very sharply.\n\nOut of nowhere, I’ll see his hand with that heavy tool again. Just that image. It can happen when I’m shaving, of all things, just standing at the sink minding my own business, or when I’m trying to work on a painting and I’m focused on something completely different. Then suddenly it’s there, like a picture popping into my head, and for a moment it doesn’t feel like an ordinary memory.\n\nMy chest tightens when it happens. I kind of freeze up inside, even if I’m still standing there in my bathroom or at the table with my paints. So yes, in that sense, I do feel like I’m back in that moment, or at least like that moment has come back into the room with me.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel like you are completely back in the moment when your brother-in-law had the heavy tool, reliving the whole scene as if it's happening again?\n\nPatient: Completely back in it… no, I don’t think I’d say it’s the whole scene like that. Not like I lose track of where I am entirely or go through the whole thing from beginning to end.\n\nIt’s more… out of nowhere I’ll see his hand again, holding that heavy tool. Just that piece of it. It comes into my mind like a picture popping up when I didn’t ask for it. I might be shaving, just doing something ordinary, or working on a painting and trying to concentrate, and then there it is. His hand, the tool, that feeling of weight to it.\n\nAnd even though I know I’m in my bathroom or sitting with my paints, my chest still tightens. I kind of freeze up inside, even if I’m still standing there in my bathroom or at the table with my paints. So yes, in that sense, I do feel like I’m back in that moment, or at least like that moment has come back into the room with me.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel like you are completely back in the moment when your brother-in-law had the heavy tool, reliving the whole scene as if it's happening again?\n\nPatient: Completely back in it… no, I don’t think I’d say it’s the whole scene like that. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Completely back… no, not completely, I don’t think. I don’t want to make it sound more dramatic than it is. I know I’m still where I am, like in the bathroom or at my painting table.

But out of nowhere, I’ll see his hand again with that heavy tool. It’s not even the whole situation from beginning to end. It’s more like one still picture that just pops into my head without warning. I can be shaving, just doing something ordinary, or working on a painting and trying to pay attention to the colors, and then there it is again.

And even though I know I’m not actually back there, my chest tightens right away. It catches me off guard, like my body remembers it before I can tell myself it’s only a memory. So I wouldn’t say I’m reliving all of it as if I’m right there, but that one image comes back strong enough that it feels very present.

At least, not that I can clearly point to.\n\nDoctor: Is there something specific about your brother-in-law or your workshop that you're trying to avoid?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I suppose there is, though I don’t always like saying it that plainly. It’s not just that I’m “not in the mood” to go out or that the workshop is messy or anything like that. Those two things—my workshop and being around my brother-in-law at family gatherings—they remind me of things I’ve been trying not to sit with.\n\nSo what I end up doing is steering around them. If there’s a birthday or holiday or someone says, “Oh, he might stop by,” I start thinking about whether I can skip it, or show up late, or leave early, or ask who exactly is coming. And with the workshop, I’ll find some excuse not to go in there, even though it used to be where I felt comfortable. I’ll tell myself I’ll do it tomorrow, or I don’t need whatever tool is in there that badly.\n\nAfter all this time, it feels almost foolish to admit how much I plan my day around avoiding those reminders. But yes, that’s really what it is. I’m trying not to run into what they bring up in me.\n\nDoctor: What exactly does your brother-in-law or the workshop remind you of that you're trying to avoid?\n\nPatient: It reminds me of… his hand, mostly. That sounds strange, maybe, because you’d think I’d say the whole situation or the argument or the room or whatever, but what comes back is his hand holding that heavy tool.\n\nIt’s not like I sit down and decide to remember it. It’ll just pop into my head out of nowhere, like a picture getting shoved in front of me. I’ll be shaving, or I’ll be working on a painting, just doing some ordinary thing, and suddenly I can see it again—his hand wrapped around it. And then my chest tightens up before I’ve even had time to think much.\n\nSo when I know he might be at a gathering, or when I think about going into the workshop where tools are sitting around, I guess I’m trying to avoid having that picture come back. Because it still does. Even now.\n\nDoctor: Was there a specific traumatic event involving your brother-in-law and the heavy tool that you're remembering?\n\nPatient: I… I don’t know how much I can lay it all out cleanly. What I can say is that what keeps coming back is that image—his hand with that heavy tool in it. That’s the part my mind seems to grab onto.\n\nIt isn’t like I’m sitting there trying to remember some whole scene from start to finish. It’s more sudden than that. I’ll be doing something ordinary, like shaving in the morning, or working on one of my paintings, and out of nowhere I’ll see it again, just like a picture popping into my head. His hand around that tool. And the strange thing is, it can happen so fast that my body reacts before I’ve really thought anything through—my chest tightens up, and I feel like I’m right back with that image for a moment.\n\nSo yes, there was something about that tool and him that stuck with me, but the part I keep remembering most clearly is that hand holding it. That’s what I’ve been trying to stay away from, I guess.\n\nDoctor: Did something bad happen involving your brother-in-law and the heavy tool?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I can say it that directly, not because I’m trying to be difficult, but because when you put it that way—“something bad happened”—my mind sort of locks up. I know what I remember, and I know what I react to. I remember him with that tool in his hand, and I remember how awful it feels when that picture comes back.\n\nBut I don’t have a clean, neat answer beyond that right now. It’s like I can get close to it, circle around it, describe the edges of it, but the minute I try to say exactly what happened, everything gets jumbled and I want to back away from it. So… I’m not saying nothing happened. I’m saying I’m not sure I can give you the full thing in words right this second.\n\nWhat I do know is that seeing him, or even thinking about that workshop, brings me right back to that image. And that’s the part I’ve been trying so hard not to face.\n\nDoctor: Did your brother-in-law use the heavy tool in a way that hurt you?\n\nPatient: I… that’s where it gets difficult for me to answer straight. I remember the shouting. I remember him having the tool, and I remember it so clearly it’s almost too clear, like that part is burned in. I can see his hand around it, the way he was standing there, the feeling in the room.\n\nBut right around how it actually ended… there’s a blank. Not the whole thing, just a piece of it. Like somebody took a few minutes and cut them out of the story. I can get up to a certain point in my mind, and then the next thing I know I’m past it somehow, but I don’t have the middle.\n\nSo when you ask if he used it in a way that hurt me, I don’t want to say something I can’t fully hold onto. I know I was scared, and I know that tool is what my mind keeps dragging back up. But the exact part where it ended, or what happened in those few minutes, is just… not there clearly.\n\nDoctor: Did your brother-in-law physically hurt you with the heavy tool?\n\nPatient: I can’t honestly say it in that exact way, because that’s the part that goes missing on me. I know that probably sounds evasive, and I’m not trying to dodge you. I remember the shouting very clearly, and I remember the tool in his hand—too clearly, really. That part is sharp in my mind.\n\nBut right around how it actually ended, there’s just this blank place. It’s like I can follow the memory up to a certain point, and then a few minutes are just gone, like somebody cut them out of the story. So when you ask if he physically hurt me with it, I don’t have a clean yes or no that I can stand behind. I remember being there, I remember him with it, I remember the noise and the fear around it… but the exact ending, the part you’re asking about, isn’t there plain enough for me to say.\n\nDoctor: Do you recall experiencing any physical pain or injury during the incident with your brother-in-law and the heavy tool?\n\nPatient: I wish I could answer that more plainly. I know you’re asking if I remember pain, like a hit or an injury right then, but that’s exactly where my memory kind of drops out.\n\nI remember the shouting. I remember the tool in his hand clear as anything—too clear, really. I can still picture that part. But right around how it actually ended, there’s this blank spot, like a few minutes got cut out of the story. I can get up to that point, and then it’s just… gone, or covered over somehow.\n\nSo I don’t want to say yes, I remember pain, because I don’t have that part clear. But I also can’t say no like I’m certain nothing happened. The part where I’d know that is the part I can’t seem to reach.\n\nDoctor: Did something traumatic happen to you on the day you remember your brother-in-law with the heavy tool?\n\nPatient: I don’t know how to answer that cleanly. I mean… something happened that day that has stayed with me, that much I can say. I remember my brother-in-law there, and I remember the heavy tool, and I remember feeling like everything in the room had gotten dangerous. It wasn’t just an ordinary argument in my mind.\n\nBut if you’re asking me to say exactly what the traumatic part was, or whether I can lay it out from start to finish, I can’t. That’s where I get stuck. I have pieces of it, and some of those pieces are very sharp, but then there’s that missing stretch where I can’t pull it together.\n\nSo… yes, in the sense that the day felt traumatic to me and it still does when I think about it. But I’m not sure I can give you the full picture of what happened, not in a way that feels honest.\n\nDoctor: Did the incident with your brother-in-law and the heavy tool involve any physical contact with you?\n\nPatient: I can’t say for certain whether there was physical contact. That’s the part that bothers me, because I feel like I ought to be able to answer a simple question like that, but I can’t honestly fill in that piece.\n\nI remember the shouting, and I remember the heavy tool in his hand plain enough. Those parts are right there, almost too clear. But right around how it actually ended, it just goes blank on me. It’s like a few minutes got cut out of the story, and that missing part is exactly where I’d be able to tell you whether he touched me or hit me or anything like that.\n\nSo I don’t want to tell you yes if I’m not sure, and I don’t want to tell you no like I know nothing happened. I just… I don’t have that part.\n\nDoctor: Do you believe that what happened with your brother-in-law is the main reason you've been avoiding your workshop and family gatherings?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think so. I mean, I don’t like saying it that directly, because then it sounds like I’ve made some neat little connection and solved it, and it doesn’t feel neat to me. But yes, that’s a big part of why I came in.\n\nI keep finding reasons not to go into my own workshop, which is ridiculous in a way, because that used to be my space. I don’t have to explain it to anybody there, I don’t have to make conversation, I can just putter around and fix things. But now I’ll stand there thinking about going in, and then I’ll tell myself I’ll do it later, or the light isn’t good, or I don’t need that tool today, or whatever excuse comes to mind. And the family gatherings are the same kind of thing, maybe worse, because if there’s even a chance my brother-in-law might be there, I start planning around it before I even admit that’s what I’m doing.\n\nAfter all this time, I’m tired of arranging my life so I don’t run into anything that reminds me of that day. I’m tired of steering clear of places and people like it’s normal. So yes, I believe what happened with him is probably the main reason, or at least the reason that makes those places feel so hard to face now.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever find yourself reliving the moment when you saw your brother-in-law with the heavy tool, like it's happening all over again?\n\nPatient: Yes… not always like I’m completely back there, if that makes sense, but there are times it comes up so strong that it feels like my body is reacting before I’ve even had a chance to think.\n\nIt’ll happen out of nowhere. Not the whole day, not a clear beginning-to-end thing, just that one picture. His hand, the weight of it, the sense of it being there. And it pops into my head like somebody slid a photograph in front of me.\n\nWhen that happens, my chest tightens up. No, not with all my senses, not like I’m fully transported back there and hearing everything and feeling the room around me. I don’t want to overstate it. I still usually know I’m in my bathroom or sitting there with my paints.

But what does happen is this one image comes in very sharply, out of nowhere. I’ll see his hand holding that heavy tool again, almost like a picture that just pops into my head without asking permission. It can be while I’m shaving, looking in the mirror, or when I’m trying to work on a painting and I’m focused on a brushstroke or a color, and then suddenly that hand is there in my mind.

And even if it isn’t the whole scene with every sound and smell and all that, my body reacts like it’s something happening right then. My chest tightens, and I have to stop for a moment. So I wouldn’t say it’s a complete reliving with all my senses, but that image feels very present when it comes.

I can be shaving in the morning, just looking at my own face in the mirror, or I’ll be working on a painting and concentrating on a color or a line, and suddenly I’ll see his hand again with that heavy tool in it. Not the whole day, not a clear beginning-to-end thing, just that one picture. His hand, the weight of it, the sense of it being there. And it pops into my head like somebody slid a photograph in front of me.\n\nWhen that happens, my chest tightens up. I’ll stop what I’m doing for a second, sometimes longer, because it doesn’t feel like just a normal memory. It has that feeling of “there it is again,” like I didn’t choose to think about it and I can’t quite push it away fast enough. So… yes, I do relive that part, at least that image of it. Not neatly, not the whole thing, but enough that it still gets to me.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel like you're back in the moment when your brother-in-law had the heavy tool, reliving it as if it's happening again?\n\nPatient: Yes… I suppose that’s the closest way to put it, though it isn’t always like I’m fully standing there again from start to finish. It’s more that one part of it comes back at me very sharply.\n\nOut of nowhere, I’ll see his hand with that heavy tool again. Just that image. It can happen when I’m shaving, of all things, just standing at the sink minding my own business, or when I’m trying to work on a painting and I’m focused on something completely different. Then suddenly it’s there, like a picture popping into my head, and for a moment it doesn’t feel like an ordinary memory.\n\nMy chest tightens when it happens. I kind of freeze up inside, even if I’m still standing there in my bathroom or at the table with my paints. So yes, in that sense, I do feel like I’m back in that moment, or at least like that moment has come back into the room with me.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel like you are completely back in the moment when your brother-in-law had the heavy tool, reliving the whole scene as if it's happening again?\n\nPatient: Completely back in it… no, I don’t think I’d say it’s the whole scene like that. Not exactly with all my senses, no. I don’t feel like I’m fully back there, like the whole thing is happening again around me. I still know I’m standing at the sink shaving, or sitting with my painting things in front of me.

But out of nowhere, I’ll get that picture in my head again — his hand with that heavy tool. It’s very clear, and it just sort of pops in without me wanting it to. I can be doing something quiet and ordinary, and then there it is. And even though I know it’s not happening right then, my chest tightens like my body reacts before I’ve had time to think it through.

So I wouldn’t say it’s a full reliving with every sense, but that one image can feel awfully strong when it comes up.

I can get up to that point, and then it’s just… gone, or covered over somehow.\n\nSo I don’t want to say yes, I remember pain, because I don’t have that part clear. But I also can’t say no like I’m certain nothing happened. The part where I’d know that is the part I can’t seem to reach.\n\nDoctor: Did something traumatic happen to you on the day you remember your brother-in-law with the heavy tool?\n\nPatient: I don’t know how to answer that cleanly. I mean… something happened that day that has stayed with me, that much I can say. I remember my brother-in-law there, and I remember the heavy tool, and I remember feeling like everything in the room had gotten dangerous. It wasn’t just an ordinary argument in my mind.\n\nBut if you’re asking me to say exactly what the traumatic part was, or whether I can lay it out from start to finish, I can’t. That’s where I get stuck. I have pieces of it, and some of those pieces are very sharp, but then there’s that missing stretch where I can’t pull it together.\n\nSo… yes, in the sense that the day felt traumatic to me and it still does when I think about it. But I’m not sure I can give you the full picture of what happened, not in a way that feels honest.\n\nDoctor: Did the incident with your brother-in-law and the heavy tool involve any physical contact with you?\n\nPatient: I can’t say for certain whether there was physical contact. That’s the part that bothers me, because I feel like I ought to be able to answer a simple question like that, but I can’t honestly fill in that piece.\n\nI remember the shouting, and I remember the heavy tool in his hand plain enough. Those parts are right there, almost too clear. But right around how it actually ended, it just goes blank on me. It’s like a few minutes got cut out of the story, and that missing part is exactly where I’d be able to tell you whether he touched me or hit me or anything like that.\n\nSo I don’t want to tell you yes if I’m not sure, and I don’t want to tell you no like I know nothing happened. I just… I don’t have that part.\n\nDoctor: Do you believe that what happened with your brother-in-law is the main reason you've been avoiding your workshop and family gatherings?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think so. I mean, I don’t like saying it that directly, because then it sounds like I’ve made some neat little connection and solved it, and it doesn’t feel neat to me. But yes, that’s a big part of why I came in.\n\nI keep finding reasons not to go into my own workshop, which is ridiculous in a way, because that used to be my space. I don’t have to explain it to anybody there, I don’t have to make conversation, I can just putter around and fix things. But now I’ll stand there thinking about going in, and then I’ll tell myself I’ll do it later, or the light isn’t good, or I don’t need that tool today, or whatever excuse comes to mind. And the family gatherings are the same kind of thing, maybe worse, because if there’s even a chance my brother-in-law might be there, I start planning around it before I even admit that’s what I’m doing.\n\nAfter all this time, I’m tired of arranging my life so I don’t run into anything that reminds me of that day. I’m tired of steering clear of places and people like it’s normal. So yes, I believe what happened with him is probably the main reason, or at least the reason that makes those places feel so hard to face now.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever find yourself reliving the moment when you saw your brother-in-law with the heavy tool, like it's happening all over again?\n\nPatient: Yes… not always like I’m completely back there, if that makes sense, but there are times it comes up so strong that it feels like my body is reacting before I’ve even had a chance to think.\n\nIt’ll happen out of nowhere. I can be shaving in the morning, just looking at my own face in the mirror, or I’ll be working on a painting and concentrating on a color or a line, and suddenly I’ll see his hand again with that heavy tool in it. Not the whole day, not a clear beginning-to-end thing, just that one picture. His hand, the weight of it, the sense of it being there. And it pops into my head like somebody slid a photograph in front of me.\n\nWhen that happens, my chest tightens up. I’ll stop what I’m doing for a second, sometimes longer, because it doesn’t feel like just a normal memory. It has that feeling of “there it is again,” like I didn’t choose to think about it and I can’t quite push it away fast enough. So… yes, I do relive that part, at least that image of it. Not neatly, not the whole thing, but enough that it still gets to me.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel like you're back in the moment when your brother-in-law had the heavy tool, reliving it as if it's happening again?\n\nPatient: Yes… I suppose that’s the closest way to put it, though it isn’t always like I’m fully standing there again from start to finish. It’s more that one part of it comes back at me very sharply.\n\nOut of nowhere, I’ll see his hand with that heavy tool again. Just that image. It can happen when I’m shaving, of all things, just standing at the sink minding my own business, or when I’m trying to work on a painting and I’m focused on something completely different. Then suddenly it’s there, like a picture popping into my head, and for a moment it doesn’t feel like an ordinary memory.\n\nMy chest tightens when it happens. I kind of freeze up inside, even if I’m still standing there in my bathroom or at the table with my paints. So yes, in that sense, I do feel like I’m back in that moment, or at least like that moment has come back into the room with me.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel like you are completely back in the moment when your brother-in-law had the heavy tool, reliving the whole scene as if it's happening again?\n\nPatient: Completely back in it… no, I don’t think I’d say it’s the whole scene like that. Not like I lose track of where I am entirely or go through the whole thing from beginning to end.\n\nIt’s more… out of nowhere I’ll see his hand again, holding that heavy tool. Just that piece of it. It comes into my mind like a picture popping up when I didn’t ask for it. I might be shaving, just doing something ordinary, or working on a painting and trying to concentrate, and then there it is. His hand, the tool, that feeling of weight to it.\n\nAnd even though I know I’m in my bathroom or sitting with my paints, my chest still tightens. So it isn’t the whole scene exactly, but that one image feels strong enough that it can knock me off balance for a bit.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel like you're completely back in the situation with your brother-in-law, reliving it all over again as if you're right there?\n\nPatient: Completely back… no, not completely, I don’t think. It’s not even the whole situation from beginning to end. It’s more like one still picture that just pops into my head without warning. I can be shaving, just doing something ordinary, or working on a painting and trying to pay attention to the colors, and then there it is again.\n\nAnd even though I know I’m not actually back there, my chest tightens right away. It catches me off guard, like my body remembers it before I can tell myself it’s only a memory. So I wouldn’t say I’m reliving all of it as if I’m right there, but that one image comes back strong enough that it feels very present.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel like you're completely back in the situation with your brother-in-law, reliving it all over again as if you're right there, with all of your senses feeling like you're actually there again?\n\nPatient: No, not with all my senses, not like I’m fully transported back there and hearing everything and feeling the room around me. I don’t want to overstate it. I still usually know I’m in my bathroom or sitting there with my paints.\n\nBut what does happen is this one image comes in very sharply, out of nowhere. I’ll see his hand holding that heavy tool again, almost like a picture that just pops into my head without asking permission. Yes… sometimes I do. Not every single night, but enough that I notice it. I’ll have a bad dream that has me back around the workshop, and it’s tied up with him and that heavy tool. It’s not always exactly like a movie replaying from start to finish, but the feeling of it is there, and when I wake up I’m just… awake. Completely awake.

Most nights I can at least get myself to bed, but I don’t settle easy. I’ll lie there listening for noises in the house, little creaks or something shifting, and my mind starts paying too much attention to it. And if I wake from one of those dreams, that’s usually it for a while. I’ll be wide awake for an hour or two, just lying there, trying to calm down enough to go back to sleep.
